1192. ELIZABETH BRACKENBURY was indicted for stealing 1 coat, value 10 s.; the goods of William Terry; and 2 candlesticks, value 2 s. 6 d., and 1 table cover, 2 s. 6 d.; the goods of John Waghorn.
WILLIAM TERRY . I am a blacksmith. I lodge at Rotherhithe—on the morning of the 22nd of April I left my room up stairs—I came down and saw the prisoner in the room down stairs—I did not know anything of her only by seeing her in the street—it was about ten minutes past six o'clock—she dropped this coat and the table-cover and candlesticks belong to Mr. John Waghorn—I was with him when he bought the table-cover.
Cross-examined by MR. DOANE. Q. What room do you occupy in this house? A. The top garrat—Mr. Waghorn is my landlord—he sleeps in the room under me—the prisoner was in the front parlour, the room that leads to the street door—Mr. Waghorn went out at five o'clock in the morning—I had left my coat in his room—Mrs. Waghorn was in bed upstairs.
THOMAS TOWEREY ( police-constable M 237.) I took the prisoner—she said she knew nothing about it—I went to Mr. Waghorn's and found this coat, candlesticks, and table-cover, chucked down inside the door.
NOT GUILTY .
[trialAccount t18460511-1193]
1193. HENRY GRAY was indicted for stealing 4 brass terrets, value 4 s., the goods of Joseph Ellis and others.
GEORGE TOLLEY . I am a saddler, and live at Richmond. I am the secretary to the Richmond Conveyance Company—these terrets are the property of the trustees of that company—Joseph Ellis is one, and there are others—I sold these terrets to Mr. Ellis two months ago—they were with the harness in the company's yard at Richmond.
JAMES WALKER . I am foreman to Mr. Joseph Ellis and others. The prisoner was horse keeper—he asked me the favour to take these terrets out of the harness and put him in some more, because these were inconvenient for him to clean—I took these out, and told him to put them by, which he did not do.
Prisoner. He told me I might have them to do what I liked with them. Witness. No such thing—I told him to take care of them—two of them are a little bent, not much.
HENRY KEENE . I deal in marine stores. On the 24th of April the prisoner brought these terrets to my shop—I thought it was suspicious—I detained them and gave information.
NOT GUILTY .
[trialAccount t18460511-1194]
1194. WILLIAM JOHN CLARK was indicted for stealing 70lbs. weight of flock, value 1 l. 15 s., and 1 bag, 1 s., the goods of Charles Seiver.
CHARLES SEIVER . I live in the London-road. About half-past six o'clock in the morning, on the 28th of April, I was called by my servant, and saw a bag of flock, which belonged to me, at Mr. Peacock's, two doors from me—I am sure it was mine—I had seen it safe in my loft the day before—the policeman had got the prisoner.
THOMAS HOLMES . I am in the prosecutor's employ. At half-past six o'clock in the morning, on the 28th of April, I went into the yard—I heard a scuffle up stairs in the warehouse, and a bag fell from the loft—two persons then jumped down from the loft—the prisoner was one—he took the bag up—I called to Burton, and he stopped him with the bag of flock.
WILLIAM BURTON . I was opening my employer's shop, which is two doors from the prosecutor's, about half-past six o'clock—I saw the prisoner and another come out of the passage leading to the prosecutor's warehouse—Holmes came out and said, "Take that man with the bag upon his back"—I stopped the prisoner with it—he said he was going to carry it for the other person, and he was going to give him a pint of beer.
Prisoner. I was standing in the Dover-road; a man asked if I wanted a job; I said, "Yes"—he told me to meet him the next morning and he would give me 6 d. to carry a bag.
COURT to HENRY HOLMES. Q. How could they get into this loft? A. There were some bricks out of the wall—they must have helped one another up.
GUILTY . Aged 18.— Confined Three Months.

1196. JANE SMITH was indicted for stealing 1 crown, 1 half-crown, 1 shilling, and 1 sixpence, the monies of George Roberts, from his person.
GEORGE ROBERTS . I am a labourer, and live at Wandsworth. On the 31st of March, between nine and ten o'clock at night, I was in the tap of the
Rose and Crown public-house—the prisoner and another woman were there—they came from Reading—both their husbands were out in the town—I treated them with what they liked to drink—I was rather drowsy, and laid my head upon the table—when I awoke up I missed this money from my left hand pocket—the prisoner had sat on that side—next day I found the woman who had been with the prisoner—I accused her of it—she said Jane Smith had taken the money out of my pocket.
ABRAHAM LOVELL . I was at the Rose and Crown with Roberts—I left him there with the woman—I never saw the money—he lent me 1 s. 6 d., and told me he had ten or a dozen shillings.
MARY ANN WITHALL . I am the wife of William Witball. On the morning of the 1st of April, about half-past five o'clock, the prisoner came to me, and asked for a cup of coffee, and change for a 5". piece—I could not change it—she went away—she returned in half an hour, and I changed half-a-crown for her.
EMMA STEVENS . I am bar-maid at the Bell Inn. On the 1st of April, at six o'clock in the morning, the prisoner changed a 5 s. piece with me—I did not give her any half-crown.
WILLIAM STONE ( police-constable F 237.) I took the prisoner—I asked if she knew anything about the money—she said no, she had not had it—next day she went before the Magistrate, and as she was going back she said, "It is no use your remanding me; I had the man's money, and all the witnesses you can bring can say nothing more"—she told me she changed the 5 s. piece at the Bell.
Prisoner. I never said sueh a thing; the man gave me the half-crown to get some beer; my husband gave me the crown to pay the rent.
NOT GUILTY .

1202. HENRY HILL was indicted for stealing 1 half-crown and lgix. pence, the monies of Edward Kent, his master.
MARY KENT . I am the wife of Edward Kent, wine-merchant, Bedford. place, Old Kent-road: the prisoner was in his service. I sent him, on the 2nd of April, for a shin of beef—he went, and brought the young man with him, with the shin of beef, and a note saying it was 3 s. 6 d.—I said I understood it was only 3 s.—the prisoner went down, and returned and said that would do—I gave him a half-crown and a sixpence, to give to Mr. Neal's servant, whose name was Paynter.
Prisoner. When I left she stopped the money for it. Witness. That was a different concern—he absconded on the 15th of April, before I had found this out—his mother came two days afterwards for his wages—Mr. Kent said he would not pay anybody but Henry—the prisoner came on the Saturday, and we deducted a half-crown that he had absconded with before.
GEORGE JOHN PAYNTER . I live with Mr. Neal. 1203. GEORGE COOPER was indicted for stealing 80 cheroots, value 6 s.; the goods of Frances Strode.
FRANCES STRODE . I am single—I live in the Triangle at Kennington-cross—a lad about the prisoner's size came to my shop last Saturday—he took a bundle of about half a pound of cheroots out with him—I have no recollection of his features.
CHARLES ROWE . I am apprentice to a shoemaker in Walworth-road—about ten o'clock, last Saturday, I was going by the Triangle at Kennington—I saw a woman on the pathway calling out "Stop thief!" and a boy running from where the cry was—he ran across the road and down Queen's-row—I ran after him till he was nearly at the top—he was stopped by the police man, and said that he was running after another person—I am of opinion he ran from the place where the woman stood—I only saw one person running—I never lost sight of him—there was a woman running, but nobody else—I did not see anything in his hand.
Cross-examined by MR. PAYNE. Q. You did not see anything throwi away? A. No—I saw him before Mr. Smith commenced running.
CHARLES JAMES SMITH . I live in Queen's-row, Kennington, about fourteen yards from Kennington-road—I was in my shop—I heard a cry of "Stop thief—I ran out and saw two persons running—I tried to stop the first—he made a leap past me—I cannot swear that it was the prisoner—he dropped 1 bundle of cigars on the ground—I distinctly saw it fall—Rowe was running after him and crying "Stop thief!"—I kept my eye on the person till he got into the arms of the policeman—though I do not recollect his person, he wai the person that Rowe was following.
Cross-examined. Q. You stooped down to pick up the bundle and then ran again? A. Yes—I did not see the prisoner's face till I got to the station—the inspector asked if I knew the lad—I said, "No"—I asked the conitt-ble if the prisoner was the person he stopped, and then I said it was him that dropped the bundle—there was nobody else in the street.
FRANCIS BANKS ( police-constable L 77.) I was on duty opposite Kennington-common
—I heard aery of "Stop thief!"—I came to the head of Queen's-row—I placed myself in a position to arrest the person who came up—the first person I saw was the prisoner, and I arrested him—Smith came up and handed me the cheroots—the prisoner said he was not the person, but he was in pursuit of another who had turned to the left—there is no turning in Queen's-row.
Cross-examined. Q. Are you sure whether he said he turned to the left or the other street? A. He said one or the other—Mr. Smith came to the station—he was asked if he knew the prisoner—he turned to me and asked if the prisoner was the person I stopped at the head of Queen's-row, and then he said he was the person who dropped the cigars.
NOT GUILTY .

1206. MARY ANN HAYES and WILLIAM MAYES were indicted for unlawfully uttering counterfeit coin.
MR. BALDWIN conducted the Prosecution.
SARAH BAUGHAN . I am the wife of John Baughao, a baker, at Nine Elms, Vauxhall. On the 4th of May, Hayes came to the shop—I am satisfied she is the. person—she asked for a penny loaf, and gave me a good half-crown—I gave her 2 s. 5 d.—she took it up, and put down a shilling and said, "This I don't like the look of"—I said, 'o No more do I; see it is a bad one, but it is not mine"—both the shillings I gave her were good ones and light ones, and the one she returned was particularly dark—she went away—the constable was at the door—he said, "Mark the shilling"—I handed it to my husband—he cut it twice with a knife, and I handed it to the policeman.
Hayes. Your husband gave me another shilling out of his waistcoat-pocket. Witness. No, he did not—he said to me," If she don't like it give her another"—I said, "It is not my shilling."
FREDERICK JAMES WEIDON . I live at the Triangle, Kennington-lane, with Mr. Knapton, a cheesemonger. On the 4th of May Hayes came to the shop about half-past two o'clock, for a 1 d. worth of cheese—I served her, and she paid me a half-crown—I gave her 2 s. 5 d.—she took it up, put down one shilling, and said she did not like the look of it—I said I did not give it her—she said, "It is all nonsense, you did"—I put it into the till, and gave her another—I had observed the shillings I gave her—they were much lighter than the one she put dowu—there was no other money in the till that I put that shilling into, but the half-crown Hayes had given me—a constable came afterwards, and I gave him the same shilling.
ELIZABETH JENNINGS . I am the wife of Edward Jennings, a baker in Holland-place, Clapham-road. On the 4th of May Hayes came and purchased a penny bun—she gave me a good half-crown—I gave her 2 s. 5 d.—she put her hand to the money, and said, 'o This is a bad shilling"—I said, "1 see it is bad, buttt is not the one I gave you"—she said, "It must be one you gave me, for I have no other money about me"—I was very particular in
giving her change, for I suspected when she came into the shop that she was coming to pass counterfeit coin, and was surprised at her giving me a good half-crown—I am quite positive the one she returned was not one of those! gave her—my husband was in the shop when she said she had no other money about her, and I said she had better be given in charge—I marked the shilling three times, with the point of a knife near the ear, and gave it to the officer—this is it.
THOMAS BENT ( police-constable V 95.) On the 5th of May I was on doty in the Wandsworth-road—I saw the two prisoners talking together a little before two o'clock—the man appeared to give her something—I then saw Hayes leave the man and cross the road, and the man sat down by some railings—I saw Hayes afterwards in Mrs. Baughan's shop, and I heard the dispute about the shilling—I received this shilling from Mrs. Baughan, and Hayes was allowed to go away—I saw her go to the end of the lane—she there joined Mayes, and they went on—I followed them to Vauxhall-gate—they stood talking some minutes, and then went towards the Triangle-May es then appeared to give her something, and Hayes went to Mr. Knap-ton's shop—she came out—then she and Mayes walked away together—I went into Knapton's and received a shilling from Weedon—I then followed the prisoners up the Clapham-road—they parted—Hayes went into a shop, and I went up to Mayes—he put something into his mouth, and held his head back—he said, "I have got nothing, I have not uttered any bad money;! have no bad money: all my money is good"—I said, "You have given the woman some"—I found on him a sovereign and a half good, and 7 1/2 d.—I found no more bad money—I found part of a penny roll, and some mould, apparently the same as had been rubbed over the shillings—It is the same colour as they are—it is road dirt, apparently mixed with gunpowder—it wai wet, but it has got quite light by being dry—the shillings given me in the shop were very dark, and appeared as if this stuff had been rubbed into the letters—this is such stuff as would darken shillings.
COURT. Q. How near was Mayes to Hayes when she wat in any shop? A. He was only three doors from the shop where she was apprehended—he wts very nearly close—at the second shop she went into, be walked by while she was in—I should think he was fifty yards from the first shop.
ROBERT MOORE ( police-sergeant V 12.) On the 4th of May I saw Hayes in Mr. Jennings's shop—I went in—Mr. Jennings gave her in charge, and Mrs. Jennings gave me this shilling—I told her to mark it.
MR. JOHN FIELD . I am inspector of coin to the Royal Mint. These shillings are all counterfeit, and cast in the same mould—they appear to hare had some black preparation rubbed over them.
Mayes's Defence. I met this young woman in Wai worth road: I asked her where she was going: she said, "To Kennington-common:" I said I would take a walk with her; I thought she was a servant; I had no bad money.
HAYES — GUILTY . Aged 20.— Confined One Year.
MAYES — NOT GUILTY .
[trialAccount t18460511-1207]
1207. ELIZABETH BAKER and MARTIN MORAN were indicted for a like offence.
MR. BALDWIN conducted the Prosecution.
JOHN THOMPSON , I am in the service of Mr. Askew, draper, Blackman-street, Borough. On the 24th of April Baker came to our shop—she bought two yards of calico, at 2 3/4 d. a yard—it came to 5 1/2 d.—she gave me a bad shilling—Mr. Askew took it from me.
JOHN ASKEW . On the 24th of April Baker came about four o'clock—I saw her served by Thompson with some calico—I saw her tender him a shilling—I took it from him, and cut it—Baker said she did not know it was bad—I told her I could not let her have it again—she put down a good shilling to pay for the calico.
Baker. I gave a half-crown. Witness. 1 did not take it—I thought it was a shilling—the officer! West, came in a little afterwards—I gave him the bad shilling—this is it.
Baker. My husband gave me a half-crown and a shilling; the shilling happened to be bad; I gave it to the shopman; he handed it to his master, and he said he should not give it back: I said I did not mind, it was no use to me if it was bad; I gave the half-crown, and he gave me two shillings and 8 halfpenny.
GEORGE THOMAS BLAKE . I am barman to Mr. Garratt, of the Ordnance Arms, in York-road, Lambeth. On the afternoon of the 24th of April the two prisoners came together—Moran asked for a pint of beerr—I drew it, and be gave me a half-crown—I put it into the till, and gave him 2 s. 4 d. change—I observed the half-crown he gave me—it was a King George III—both the prisoners drank the beer, and went away—West came in afterwards, and I looked at the half-crown I had received from Moran—it was bad—there I was only one other half-crown in. the till—that was under the shillings and six pences, and that was a Victoria half-crown—between the time of Moran giving me the half-crown arid my looking at it again, I had not put any money io the till, and no one had interfered with the till at all—I marked the half-crown—thia is it.
THOMAS WEST ( police-constable M 249.) On the 24th of April. on duty about two o'clock in the day at Newington—causeway—I saw the two prisoners—Moran walked a little ahead of Baker—I followed them—Baker joined him, and they conversed together—they west on about half-mile beyond the Elephant and Castle—they returned in the same direction—I saw Baker go into Askew's shop, and put down the half-crown—Moran stopped six or eight yards from the door, on the same side of the way—he could not command a view of the shop—he could only see the door.
COURT. Q. Did he linger at all about the shop? A. He stopped at the door about a minute—Baker went in just as he passed on—he stopped six or eight yards past the door—I went into the shop, aad when I came out I saw them in company together, walking away, about twenty yards from the shop.
MR. BALDWIN. Q. Where did they go? A. To the Elephant and Castle aad to the London-road, and they went to the Ordnance Arms—I followed them to the door—I saw them come out—I wtntin and saw Blake, he looked in the till and saw the half-crown—I told him to mark it—I went after the prisoners and came up with them at the foot of Westminster-bridge—they were together—I took the man—the woman left, and I sent another officer to take her—I told the man what I took him for, he said he knew nothing about it—he resisted me.
JOHN WHITLAMB ( police-constable M 89.) On the 24th of April I saw the prisoners in Newington-causeway—I followed them—I saw the woman go to Mr. Askew's shop—the man was at one time within ten yards of thd door, she was in the shop, and then he crossed and came on the same side as I was, then went and stood against the fourth cab on the rank—he could see the shop door—I followed them to the Ordnance Arms, and received this bad half-crown from Blake.
WILLIAM MANCOK ( police-constable B 149.) I was on duty, and West called me to assist in taking Baker—I found on Moran two counterfeit halfcrowns
in his jacket-pocket, and 6 s. 6 d. in silver, and 3 d. in copper, money—7 1/4 d. good was found on Baker.
MR. JOHN FIELD . These three half-crowns and the shilling are all counterfeit.
Moraris Defence. This is my wife, but she was confused and gave her maiden name; I got change for a sovereign in the Borough-market; I gave her a half-crown and a shilling to buy something she wanted.
BAKER — NOT GUILTY .
MORAN — GUILTY . Aged 28.— Confined One Year.
Before Mr. Common Sergeant.

1208. WILLIAM RICKETTS and DANIEL JOHNSON were indicted for stealing 19 live tame fowls the property of William Leeson; and that Johnson had been before convicted of felony.
MARY BISHOP . I am servant to Mr. William Leeson of Loughborougb-road, North Brixton. On the night of the 30th of April I went to theheo-house in consequence of the policeman coming—I found there was nothing there—I had been in the yard about four o'clock, and saw the nineteen fowls all safe, and I fed them—at half-past nine they were all gone—they were Mr. William Leeson's—here are two of them here, and part of a dead one.
Ricketts. Q. How do you swear to them? A. By the rings round tbeir necks—we bred them—I know them perfectly well—I will swear to them.
GEORGE WESTON ( police-constable P 205.) I was on duty about five minutes past nine o'clock at night, on the 30th of April, and saw the prisoners, Johnson had a bag on his shoulder, and Ricketts a bag under his arm—I followed them till we came to another officer, and we took them both—Ricketts' bag had seven fowls in it, and Johnson's had eight, and each of the prisoner! had two live fowls in his jacket pocket.
Ricketts' Defence. We met a man who gave us these fowls to carry to the Elephant and Castle, and said he would give us a shilling.

1209. MARY STEVENS was indicted for stealing 1 watch, value 2 l.; 1 half-crown, 34 shillings, 3 sixpences, 2 groats, 1 penny, and 3 halfpence, the property of Edward John Toby, from his person.
EDWARD JOHN TOBY . At half-past twelve o'clock at night, I returned with my master's horse and cart to his brewery at Lambeth—the prisoner followed me into the stable and asked me how I was—I told her I was very well, and to go about her business—I went to look for the ostler, but did not see him—I went back to the stable and the prisoner was gone—I sat down, being very tired, and went to sleep—there are two doors to the stable—I had shut the bottom one—some time after I awoke—I looked for my watch and it was gone—I then missed my money, a half-crown, thirty-four shillings, three sixpences, two groats, and 2% d.—I ran out and met a constable—I asked if he had seen a person dressed as I described the prisoner to be—he said he bad, and he went alter her—she saw him and ran away—she drew this watch from her bosom—it is mine—I had been drinking, but was not drunk—I sleep w a bed opposite the brewery—I had not seen the prisoner in the stable after she asked me how I was.
Prisoner. I met him; he took me to the premises, and dragged me in; he fastened the door and took me into a cellar: he drew several mugs of ale from the barrels; I asked him to let me out; he said, "No, I have got you in and you shall stop;" he began to take very improper liberties with me; he said, "I will give you all I have got;" he put his watch in my bosom—I then had a dreadful fall, and when I awoke up he was by my side, and he gave me into custody.
Witness. I drove the horse and cart to the yard—I could not get into the brewery at all—she was taken a mile and a half from the place.
JOHN MAY ( police-constable V 218.) T met the prosecutor in the Wands-worth-road—he asked if I had seen such a person as he described—I said I had 20 minutes before, going towards Vauxhall-gate—he said she had robbed him of his watch and money—I saw the prisoner, she ran 20 or 30 yards, and I took her—I said it was for stealing a young man's watch and money—she said, "He gave it me"—I found on her 1 l. 17 s. 8 d. in silver, and 2 1/2 d. in copper, and at the station I found a shilling in her right boot—she took this watch out of her bosom and gave it me.
Prisoner's Defence. I had no intention of keeping it, if I had I should have gone home.
NOT GUILTY .

1211. HARRIET EDWARDS was indicted for stealing 1 watch-chain, 1 s.; 1 watch-key, 3 d.; I seal, 9 d.: the goods of George Patterson, from his person: and that she had been before convicted of felony.
MR. ROBINSON conducted the Prosecution.
GEORGE PATTERSON . I am a gardener, and live at Clapham. On the 11th of May I went to the William the Fourth, in the Wandsworth-road, to have half-a-pint of beer—I left there about five o'clock in the afternoon, and was going home—I met a young woman who came smiling and looking very pleasant to me—I thought it was some one who knew me, but I could not think who it was—she came quite close to me, squeezed herself up against me—I began to think she was a bad girl, and I thought I felt something at my watch—I put down my hand to feel if my wateh was safe, and my chain, and key, and seal, were gone—I said, o'You have robbed me of my watch"—she said, "No"—I said, "You have"—I laid hold of her, and she gave me a good blow on my cheek—I dragged her back to the William the Fourth, which was only about fifty yards off—I asked the landlord if he would send for a policeman—when I got the young woman within the public-house door, she gave me the chain"—she held out her hand to me, and I took it out of her hand—I observed that the ring was broken at the end of the chain which had fastened it to the watch—I found my watch was in my pocket—I turned the pocket inside out
and found the broken ring, which was between the watch and the chain—the chain was not broken—I do not know the young woman—I never saw her before to my knowledge—T see nobody more like her than the prisoner is.
Cross-examined by MR. PARRY. Q. What were you doing there? A. I was reading the newspaper—the young woman met me sibout eighty yards from the William the Fourth, in the path by the road—I cannot say whether any persons were passing when she came up to me—I took no liberties at all with her.
MARY FRANCIS . I am single, and live in Thurlow-cottage, Wandsworth, On the 11th of May I saw the prosecutor in the road about five o'clock—I saw a woman walk up to him as if she knew him—she had a brown gown on with two flounces—I did not see her face—I walked on.
Cross-examined. Q. You saw no scuffle, nor heard any? A. No—the young woman was on the right side of the road—she crossed to the left, and went up to the prosecutor—he did not seem as if he knew her—his head was down at the time—I went into the linen-draper's where I was going.
JOSEPH PERKINS . I am ostler at the William the Fourth. On the 11th of May I stood at the door as the prosecutor went out—I then heard a noise—I turned my head, and saw his hat and wig fall off—I went up, and the prisoner and he were scuffling together—the prosecutor called out, "Police!"—the prisoner did not make any resistance when she saw me looking towards them—the prosecutor had hold of her hand, and said she had robbed him of his watch—they then walked down quietly to where I was—he had hold of her hand all the time—the prisoner said he had accused her of robbing him of his watch, and, if there was a policeman, she would give him in charge—I picked up his wig, and went for a policeman—when I came back, he was pulling the watch out.
Cross-examined. Q. Did they go into the public-house? A. Yes, jurt inside the door—when I first saw them, they were between thirty and forty yards from me—the prisoner made no resistance after I went in sight—they were tusseling together when I first saw them—they went into the public-house—they had nothing to drink—the prisoner laughed outside, and said this old man had accused her of robbing him of his watch—I did not hear what took place inside—I went away to look for the wig, leaving them in the public-house.
GEORGE AUSTIN . I am the landlord of the William the Fourth, in the Wands worth-road—on the 11th of May I was called down stairs by the bar maid, who said an old man had been robbed—I came down, and old Patterson was at the bar with the prisoner—he said he had been robbed of his watch and he had the chain in his hand—I requested him to look for his watch—he looked in his fob, found it and took it out—the prisoner denied the robbery, and said, "The old man has his watch"—I sent the ostler for a police man, and during his absence I looked at the chain to see how it was broken off—I saw there was no ring, and I desired the old man to look in his pocket—he turned it out and took out this piece of a ring—I gave the prisoner in custody.
GEORGE PATTERSON re-examined. This is the ring that was to my chain and watch, and this my watch-chain.
WILLIAM GLEED ( police-constable V 276.) I took the prisoner into custody.
JOHN LONG . I am a working man, and live in the Wands worth-road—I did not see the first of this, but I saw the prisoner give the chain and seal and key to the old gentleman—these are them.
GEORGE AUSTIN re-examined. Q. Did you see the prosecutor and the prisoner in front of the bar? A. Yes, the prisoner was laughing, and said, "The
old man says I robbed him, but you see he has got his watch"—she wished to go away, but I detained her.
GEORGE WILD ( police-constable M 94.) I produce a certificate of the prisoner's former conviction at Newington—( read—Convicted 18th March, 1844, and confined twelve months)—the prisoner is the person.
* GUILTY . Aged 20.— Transported for Ten Years.
[trialAccount t18460511-1212]
1212. RICHARD WESTON was indicted for stealing 1 saw, value 6 d. the goods of John Scargall.
JOHN SCARGALL . I live in Windmill-court, High-street, Lambeth, and am a carpenter—I became acquainted with the prisoner about six months ago—he came to live with me—I missed a great many articles, but I did not miss this saw till I saw it for sale, where I was looking for some other things—this is my saw—I missed thirty or forty moulding planes.
ELEANOR DYER . I bought this saw of the prisoner—he offered some other things for sale.
Prisoner. Q. What day did you buy that of me? A. I cannot say—it was about the early part of April.
GUILTY . Aged 22.— Confined Six Months.
[trialAccount t18460511-1213]
1213. JOHN GREEN was indicted for stealing 5 live tame pigeons, price 7 s. the property of Sidney Wilson.
SIDNEY WILSON . I live at the Paragon, Francis-street, Newington, and am a butcher. On the 5th of May I lost seven pigeons—here are two of them here that I can swear to.
Cross-examined by MR. PLATT. Q. Which are those you speak to? A. These two—I pulled five feathers out of the wing of this one, and they have grown again—I breed pigeons, but I did not breed this one—this other one I will swear to by the marks upon the back and the beard—on one side is dark, and the other light—on the hip of it there were some red feathers, and there are some there now—one of these is what is called a soft feathered bird—the other is a black mottled—they were not in the habit of flying about, they will sometimes—they have not been thrown up—they were in a shed—I saw them safe about eight o'clock in the evening, and missed them by five o'clock the next morning.
EDWARD CLARK ( police-constable P 162.) I produce one of these pigeons—I found it in the prisoner's house on the 9th of May—he said he had bought some, but he could not give an account of this one.
Cross-examined. Q. Point out the one the prisoner could give no account of? A. This black one—I found four other pigeons there—he does not deal in pigeons—he went very quietly when I took him—I asked him if his name was Green—he said, "Yes"—I asked what he had in the bag, and he said, "Pigeons"—he was at first liberated, because I had not had time to go round to know if the pigeons were stolen.
RICHARD DAVIS ( police-constable P 55.) I found this other pigeon in a cage in the prisoner's room—he said it waft his own, and he, had had it a long time.
(The prisoner received a good character.)
GUILTY .— Aged 20.— Confined Twelve Months.
ADJOURNED TO THE 15 TH OF JUNE.
